```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0001
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 309c-309d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt
  stephanus_span: 309c-309d
  start_marker: 309c
  end_marker: 309d
  start_char: 714
  end_char: 1330
  text_sha256: 5a9617cb402ca1c844deff62b1d533b1ba722df867d417c76462cf7ce54541f6
greek_terms:
  - σοφώτατον
  - σοφωτάτῳ
  - σοφώτατος
  - καλλίων
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_1443
feature_label: socratic_deflection_via_superlative
observation: "Socrates deflects the question about the stranger's beauty by recoding it as a question about wisdom. When the Companion asks how the stranger could appear more beautiful than Alcibiades, Socrates answers that the wisest must appear more beautiful. He then uses three successive superlatives of sophos (to sophotaton at 309c, sophotatoi at 309d, sophotatos at 309d) to characterize Protagoras, pushing the valuation language from beauty to wisdom without denying the premise."
textual_basis: "At 309c the Companion asks whether the stranger is an Athenian or a foreigner and then why he would appear more beautiful (kallion) than Alcibiades. Socrates answers with a rhetorical question that shifts the evaluative domain: the wisest must appear more beautiful. By 309d he has identified the stranger as Protagoras and called him wisest of our contemporaries, using three superlative forms in rapid succession."
limits: "This observation does not prove Socrates is being ironic. It registers the structural deflection from beauty to wisdom and the concentration of superlative terminology. Whether this marks Socratic irony, genuine admiration for Protagoras, or both is not settled by the text at this point."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1493
feature_label: erotic_hunt_metaphor_opening
observation: The dialogue opens with a companion asking Socrates whether he has come from hunting the youthful bloom of Alcibiades. The opening question frames the dramatic situation in erotic-pursuit language before any philosophical content appears.
textual_basis: At 309a the opening exchange uses the metaphor of a hunt (kynegesion) targeting Alcibiades' youthful beauty (hora) as the framing device for Socrates' entrance. The companion asks where Socrates is appearing from and immediately answers his own question with the erotic-hunt suggestion.
limits: This observation does not interpret the metaphor as esoteric or psychological. It only notes that the dramatic frame uses erotic-pursuit language. The companion remains unnamed in this segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: prosopography
feature_id: feature_candidate_1494
feature_label: homeric_authority_cited_in_dialogue
observation: Socrates identifies his interlocutor as an admirer of Homer and deploys a Homeric citation about the charm of youth at the first growth of beard to deflect the companion's remark about Alcibiades' age.
textual_basis: At 309a-309b Socrates calls the companion a praiser of Homer and quotes the Homeric claim that the most graceful youth is that of the first beard-growth, applying it to Alcibiades' present age. The interlocutor is characterized by his stance toward Homer, and the citation serves as a rejoinder in the banter about Alcibiades.
limits: This observation does not identify the interlocutor by name (he is not named in this segment) and does not claim the Homeric citation carries hidden meaning. It only notes the dramatic use of Homeric authority as a social marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0004

greek_terms: ["ἄτοπον", "προσεῖχον τὸν νοῦν", "ἐπελανθανόμην"]
english_gloss: "strange thing, I paid no attention, I kept forgetting him"
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_1495
feature_label: socratic_self_deprecation_forgetfulness
observation: Socrates reports a strange thing: although Alcibiades was present, Socrates paid no attention to him and repeatedly forgot about him. This self-deprecating claim about forgetting a famously beautiful youth subverts the erotic-hunt frame just established.
textual_basis: At 309b Socrates announces he wants to tell the companion something strange (atopon): while Alcibiades was present, Socrates did not pay attention to him and frequently forgot him. The claim that he forgot Alcibiades while Alcibiades was present undermines the companion's opening suggestion that Socrates is engaged in erotic pursuit.
limits: This observation does not claim Socrates is being insincere or that this is a hidden doctrinal signal. It notes the textual irony of Socrates denying attention to the very person the dramatic frame highlights.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: embedded_narrated_sequence
observation: >
  Hippocrates narrates the sequence of events that delayed his arrival:
  his slave Satyrus ran away, he was about to report this but forgot due to
  some other matter, they dined after his return, and only then did his
  brother mention Protagoras's arrival; he attempted to go to Socrates
  immediately but judged the night too far along, and came at dawn once
  sleep released him from fatigue.
textual_basis: >
  At 310c Hippocrates recounts the chain of causes — the runaway slave
  (Satyrus), the forgotten report, the delayed dinner conversation — before
  reaching the mention of Protagoras at 310c-d. The narrator embeds
  Hippocrates's self-report within Socrates's first-person frame, adding a
  layer of reported speech within the dialogue frame.
limits: >
  Does not by itself prove a systematic frame-nesting strategy; only one
  embedded narrative sequence is present here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0006
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 310c-310d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt
  stephanus_span: 310c-310d
  start_marker: 310c
  end_marker: 310d
  start_char: 2253
  end_char: 3160
  text_sha256: 12bb2bb8526796ca455b8faa6363f4edfa0633552f0f1c617e5dcff603ae2926
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_1562
feature_label: socratic_feigned_ignorance
observation: >
  When Hippocrates bursts in and speaks with agitation about Protagoras,
  Socrates asks whether Protagoras has done him some injustice (môn ti se
  adikei Prôtagoras;). Hippocrates laughs and replies that Protagoras is
  wise and does not make him wise. Socrates then responds with a practical
  suggestion — give him money and persuade him, and he will make you wise
  too — delivered with the oath nai ma Dia.
textual_basis: >
  At 310d Socrates frames Hippocrates's excitement about the sophist as a
  possible injury, using the legal term adikei (does wrong/injustice). The
  question reads as a deflationary move: treating the desire for wisdom as
  a grievance. Hippocrates's laughter (gelasas) signals he recognizes the
  mismatch between Socrates's framing and his own enthusiasm, while
  Socrates's earnest-seeming reply (nai ma Dia) offers a remedy in
  transactional terms.
limits: >
  Does not prove Socrates is being ironic; the exchange is consistent with
  either genuine naïveté or playful irony. Only the surface textual markers
  — the legal framing of a non-legal matter and the interlocutor's laughter
  — are recorded.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  At 310c Hippocrates says ho gar toi pais me ho Satyros apedra — "my
  slave Satyrus ran away." The slave is given a personal name (Satyrus)
  rather than being mentioned only by status term. The name appears in a
  casual domestic narrative, not a philosophical argument.
limits: >
  Does not establish that named slaves are a systematic Platonic feature;
  only one instance in this segment. The dramatic function of the name is
  not evaluated here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0017
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 312c-312d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt
  stephanus_span: 312c-312d
  start_marker: 312c
  end_marker: 312d
  start_char: 6476
  end_char: 7458
  text_sha256: 7755473db492b07d7e8dd7a60ee71efb3cb22778d8d02ff8d8fa448a71bce536
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1692
feature_label: definition_by_name_etymology
observation: |
  Hippocrates defines the sophist by appeal to the name itself: the sophist is one who knows wise things (ton ton sophon epistemon). Socrates immediately tests this by noting that the same formula applies to painters and carpenters, who are also knowers of wise things in their respective domains.
textual_basis: |
  At 312c, asked to say what the sophist is, Hippocrates replies "just as the name says (hosper tounoma legei), this one is the knower of wise things (ton ton sophon epist\u0113mona)." Socrates then extends the same formula to painters and carpenters (312c-312d), showing the definition is too broad.
limits: |
  The observation records the appeal to etymology as a definitional move and Socrates' immediate test by domain extension. It does not assess whether the name-based definition is sincere or ironized.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0018
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 312c-312d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt
  stephanus_span: 312c-312d
  start_marker: 312c
  end_marker: 312d
  start_char: 6476
  end_char: 7458
  text_sha256: 7755473db492b07d7e8dd7a60ee71efb3cb22778d8d02ff8d8fa448a71bce536
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_087
feature_label: definition_revised_after_objection
observation: |
  When Socrates shows that the name-based definition (knower of wise things) is too broad, Hippocrates offers a revised definition: the sophist is an overseer (epistates) of making someone clever at speaking (deinon legein). Socrates accepts it as possibly true but not sufficient, and presses for the subject matter about which the sophist makes one clever at speaking.
textual_basis: |
  After Socrates extends the etymological formula to painters and carpenters at 312c-312d, Hippocrates replies with a new definition at 312d: the sophist is an epistates of making one clever at speaking. Socrates then demands further specification by analogy with the lyre-player (kitharistes).
limits: |
  This records the pattern of definition-revision triggered by an objection about overbreadth. It does not evaluate whether the revised definition is adequate or whether Socrates' demand for specification is itself a rhetorical move.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0019

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1083
feature_label: refutation_by_counterexample
observation: |
  Socrates refutes Hippocrates' initial name-based definition of the sophist by showing that the formula "knower of wise things" applies equally to painters and carpenters, whose crafts are distinct from that of the sophist. The counterexample establishes that the definition fails to distinguish the sophist's specific domain.
textual_basis: |
  At 312c-312d, Socrates states that the formula "knower of wise things" can also be said of painters (zographon) and carpenters (tektonon). He then asks: if someone inquired "of what sort of wise things are painters knowers?" the answer would be "those concerning the production of images." The sophist's domain remains unspecified.
limits: |
  This records the structure of refutation by showing that a definition applies to cases outside the intended target. It does not claim the refutation is decisive or that Hippocrates recognizes it as such.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0022
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 312e-313a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt

  end_marker: 313a
  start_char: 7458
  end_char: 8277
  text_sha256: f6cdc9c6f3683a64ebda2c574b6ccab667f2822d77a6563a40d5666ae04de4e9
greek_terms:
  - πατρὶ
  - ἀδελφῷ
english_gloss: "father, brother"
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1700
feature_label: elenctic_failure_to_answer
observation: "Hippocrates is pressed to identify the sophist's knowledge domain through an analogy with the kithara-player and reaches an inability to answer. This is a standard elenctic endpoint: the respondent is given a seemingly clear analogical framework and still cannot produce the definition, revealing that he does not know what he thought he knew."
textual_basis: "At 312e, Socrates sets up the analogy from the kithara-player (someone clever at speaking about kithara-playing, the subject he knows) and asks Hippocrates to apply it to the sophist. When Hippocrates agrees with the analogical structure but cannot fill in the specific knowledge domain, he swears by Zeus and admits he has nothing more to say. This is a classic elenctic breakdown: agreement to a framework followed by inability to supply the content."
limits: "This observation identifies the elenctic breakdown pattern. It does not claim that Hippocrates has been refuted on a specific proposition or that the elenchus ends here."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - πωλοῦντες
  - καπηλεύοντες
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_1727
feature_label: sophist_merchant_analogy
observation: Socrates compares sophists who travel from city to city selling teachings to merchants who sell goods for the body. Just as merchants praise all their wares without knowing which are beneficial or harmful for the body, sophists praise all their teachings without knowing which are beneficial or harmful for the soul.
textual_basis: At 313d Socrates explicitly draws the comparison: just as merchants do not themselves know what is beneficial or harmful for the body, so too those who peddle teachings around the cities and sell and retail them to whoever desires them praise everything they sell, but some of them may be ignorant of what they sell whether it is beneficial or harmful for the soul.
limits: This observation records the analogy as stated; it does not evaluate whether the comparison is fair to Protagoras or whether Socrates accepts it as definitive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1589
feature_label: psychic_somatic_craft_pairing
observation: Socrates constructs a structural parallel between bodily expertise and psychic expertise. Just as one needs a trainer or doctor to judge what is beneficial or harmful for the body, one needs analogous expertise to judge what teachings are beneficial or harmful for the soul.
textual_basis: At 313d-313e the parallel is explicit: the gymnastikos or iatros is the expert for the body; the iatrikos concerning the soul is the corresponding expert for the soul, introduced by the phrase peri tên psychên au iatrikos.
limits: This observation records the structural parallel; it does not claim that Socrates asserts such soul-expertise actually exists or that he himself possesses it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: dramatic_case_setup
feature_id: feature_candidate_1550
feature_label: protreptic_stakes_announced
observation: Socrates presents the decision to study with Protagoras as a matter of risk for the soul. If Hippocrates is knowledgeable about what is beneficial and harmful for the soul, it is safe to purchase teachings from Protagoras or anyone else; if not, he should beware.
textual_basis: At 313e Socrates states the conditional explicitly: if Hippocrates happens to be knowledgeable (epistēmōn) about what is beneficial (chrēston) and harmful (ponēron), it is safe (asphales) to buy teachings; but if not, he should watch out (hora), addressing him as ō makarie.
limits: This observation records the stakes as Socrates frames them; it does not determine whether Hippocrates actually lacks such knowledge or whether the warning is ultimately justified.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0027

greek_terms: ["σωμα", "ψυχῇ"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_043
feature_label: expert_craft_analogy
observation: "Socrates draws an extended analogy between buying food and buying teaching (314a-314b). Food purchased from a merchant can be taken home and examined by an expert before consumption; teaching must be received directly into the soul and cannot be inspected beforehand."
textual_basis: "Socrates contrasts the purchase of food and drink (sitia, pota) from a retailer (kapēlos, emporos), which can be removed in other vessels and consulted on with an expert (epaïonta), with learning (mathēmata), which cannot be carried off in another vessel but goes directly into the soul (314a-314b)."
limits: "The analogy identifies a structural asymmetry between bodily and psychic nourishment but does not claim that teaching is a technē in the same way that food-inspection is. It frames the risk of sophistry without evaluating it."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates asks Hippocrates whether he would not be ashamed to present himself to the Greeks as a sophist, a question that simultaneously tests Hippocrates' self-understanding and exposes the social stigma attached to the label sophist."
textual_basis: "When Hippocrates blushes (312a) and hesitantly admits he intends to become a sophist, Socrates follows with an oath-laden question: 'by the gods, would you not be ashamed to present yourself to the Greeks as a sophist?' The question is not a neutral inquiry but a pointed provocation that forces Hippocrates to confront the public perception of sophists."
limits: "Does not establish Socrates' own view of sophistry; only shows his rhetorical tactic of using shame as a diagnostic probe."
```

```yaml
feature_label: craft_vs_liberal_education_distinction
observation: "Socrates introduces a distinction between learning something as a craft (techne) to become a professional craftsman (demiourgos) and learning something for the sake of liberal education (paideia) as befits a private citizen and free man."
textual_basis: "At 312b, Socrates contrasts Hippocrates' prior learning with the grammatistes, kitharistes, and paidotribes — which Hippocrates studied not for a craft but for paideia, 'as befits the private man and the free man' — with the uncertain character of study with Protagoras. The craft/liberal-education distinction is explicit: each subject was learned 'not for a craft, so as to become a craftsman, but for education.'"
limits: "The observation records the distinction as posed by Socrates; it does not adjudicate whether Protagoras' teaching falls on one side or the other, which the dialogue has not yet resolved at this point."
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates identifies Protagoras's professional identity by a craft-name analogy: just as Pheidias is called a sculptor and Homer a poet, so Protagoras is called something by virtue of his craft. The analogy uses named exemplars whose professional title is derived from their craft domain.
textual_basis: At 311e, Socrates asks "what name do we hear spoken about Protagoras?" and immediately supplies the pattern: "just as about Pheidias [we hear] sculptor and about Homer poet, what such thing do we hear about Protagoras?" The structure pairs a named individual with a craft-derived professional title to elicit the corresponding title for Protagoras.
limits: This observation records the craft-name analogy pattern only; it does not assess whether "sophist" genuinely names a craft. It does not claim the analogy is valid or invalid.
```

```yaml
observation: Hippocrates reports that Protagoras is called a sophist by professional designation. Socrates then confirms the transactional implication: they are going to pay money to him as a sophist.
textual_basis: At 311e, Hippocrates answers Socrates's craft-name question with "they call the man a sophist" (sophisten... onomazousi... ton andra einai). Socrates immediately draws the practical consequence: "So we are going to pay the money to him as a sophist?" (hos sophistei ara erchometha telountes ta chremata), and Hippocrates confirms.
limits: This observation records only that Protagoras is named by the trade-designation "sophist" within the dramatic frame. It does not evaluate whether the designation is accurate or what "sophist" entails.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: This observation records the transactional framing of the sophist encounter only. It does not assess whether Protagoras actually charged fees, nor does it evaluate the ethics of paying for instruction.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 314c-314d Socrates reports standing in the doorway until the road discussion is completed; the doorkeeper, a eunuch, overhears, identifies them as sophists, and closes the door."
limits: "This records the entry-barrier scene at Callias' house. It does not treat the doorkeeper's reaction as a general judgment on sophistry beyond the local action."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  be Pausanias's beloved (ouk an thaumazoimi ei paidika Pausaniou tynchanei
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1930
feature_label: ironic_admiration_of_sophist
observation: |
  Socrates reports that he was eager to listen to Prodicus (315e), calling
  him a man who seems to him exceedingly wise (passophos). He notes this
  despite being unable to make out what Prodicus was saying from outside the
  room.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates says he could not learn what they were discussing (ouk edynamen
  ego ge mathein exothen) yet was eager to hear Prodicus (liperos echon
  akouein tou Prodikou) because the man seems to him exceedingly wise
  (passophos gar moi dokei haner, 315e).
limits: |
  Does not assert that Socrates is being insincere. Records only the
  juxtaposition of unfulfilled eagerness with an extreme praise term, a
  pattern consistent with Socratic irony markers elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: social_odium_as_concealment_motive
observation: "Protagoras attributes the historical concealment of sophistry to fear of social resentment: practitioners feared envy (phthonos) and used the arts as a protective screen."
textual_basis: "At 316d-316e Protagoras states that ancient practitioners feared the odium (to epachthes) of sophistry, made a screen out of fear, and used the arts as curtains because they feared envy (phthonon). This establishes the social stakes that frame Protagoras's own contrary practice of openly professing sophistry."
limits: "This observation records the motive attribution. It does not assess whether Protagoras's historical claim about his predecessors' fear is accurate, nor does it evaluate his self-positioning as more courageous."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: expert_craft_analogy
observation: Socrates uses named craft practitioners as analogical foils to demand that Protagoras specify what domain his teaching improves. Zeuxippus the Heraclean is cited as a teacher of painting, Orthagoras the Theban as a teacher of flute-playing.
textual_basis: At 318b-c, Socrates constructs a comparison: if Hippocrates were to study with Zeuxippus, the answer to "in what does one become better?" would be "in painting"; if with Orthagoras, "in flute-playing." These named practitioners serve as craft-teaching exemplars against which Protagoras is asked to define his own teaching domain.
limits: This observation notes the use of craft practitioners as analogical foils. It does not evaluate whether the analogy is apt or whether Protagoras's teaching qualifies as a craft.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 318b-c, Socrates frames the inquiry with an "as if" construction: if Hippocrates suddenly changed his desire and sought association with Zeuxippus or Orthagoras instead, he would receive a domain-specific answer (painting or flute-playing). Socrates then applies this hypothetical structure to demand a parallel answer from Protagoras.
limits: This observation notes the hypothetical construction as a procedural device. It does not assess the logical validity of the analogy or predict Protagoras's response.

```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 318b-c, Socrates frames the demand with the question form: "in what do you say he will become better, and toward what will he make progress?" This marks the content of Protagoras's teaching as the target of inquiry, parallel to how painting and flute-playing specify the domains of Zeuxippus and Orthagoras.
```

```yaml
feature_label: thesis_stated_in_opening_exchange
observation: "Protagoras states his professional undertaking (epangelma) explicitly: the political craft (politike techne) that makes men good citizens. Socrates then names the question that will structure the subsequent argument: whether this is teachable."
textual_basis: "At 319a Protagoras says: 'this is exactly it, Socrates, the undertaking I undertake' (auto men oun touto estin, to epangelma ho epangellomai). Socrates responds by raising the teachability question (319a-319b, ouk oimen didakton einai)."
limits: "This record notes the exchange that frames the dialogue's central question. It does not evaluate whether the political craft is in fact teachable or assess Socrates' later arguments."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: mock_deference
observation: "Socrates calls Protagoras' professional undertaking a 'fine craft-possession' (kalon techne-ma) but immediately hedges with 'if indeed you have acquired it' (eiper kektēsai), then adds that his own view is that political excellence is not teachable. He pairs apparent admiration with fundamental doubt."
textual_basis: "319a: 'A fine craft-possession indeed you have acquired, if indeed you have acquired it' (ē kalon techne-ma ara kektēsai, eiper kektēsai). Socrates then states his own contrary belief (319a-319b, ouk ōimēn didakton einai). The conditional eiper suspends the compliment."
limits: "This observation identifies a single instance of mock-deference in the prologue. It does not claim that all Socratic compliments to Protagoras are ironic."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 319c, Socrates describes how Athenians mock and forcibly remove unqualified speakers in technical matters. observation: "Socrates describes the Athenian assembly's enforcement mechanism at 319c: when an unqualified speaker attempts to advise on technical matters, the crowd laughs, shouts him down, and the Scythian archers drag him away on the prytaneis' orders."
```
```yaml
textual_basis: "At 319c, Socrates narrates that when someone deemed a non-expert tries to give advice on technical subjects, the Athenians laugh and create an uproar until the speaker either withdraws, shouted down, or the archers drag him off or remove him at the prytaneis' command."
limits: "This describes assembly practice for technical matters, not political deliberation, where Socrates says no such enforcement occurs. It does not independently verify whether Scythian archers performed this function historically."
review_status: accepted
```

Socrates labels himself and his companions as erotic admirers of Protagoras."
```yaml
textual_basis: "At 317c-d Socrates says he suspected (hypopteusa) that Protagoras wanted to show off and preen (kallopisasthai) to Prodicus and Hippias that erastai of his had arrived, and he includes himself in that group with the first-person plural verb eimen."
limits: "Socrates reports his own suspicion, not a confirmed fact about Protagoras' motive. The erastes language is Socrates' characterization, not necessarily Protagoras' own view."
```

```yaml
  - κάμπτομαι
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_260
feature_label: mock_deference
observation: |
  Socrates claims he is swayed (320b) toward Protagoras's view that virtue is teachable because he regards Protagoras as experienced, learned, and inventive, immediately after listing counterexamples of virtuous men who failed to teach virtue.
textual_basis: |
  At 320b Socrates says he does not think virtue is teachable based on the evidence he has just cited, then states he is bending or being swayed upon hearing Protagoras speak, attributing his shift to Protagoras's reputation for experience, learning, and discovery. The juxtaposition of a strongly argued case against teachability with a sudden concession based on the interlocutor's reputation signals mock-deference rather than genuine persuasion.
limits: |
  Does not determine whether Socrates is genuinely open to being persuaded. Does not assess whether Protagoras actually possesses the expertise Socrates attributes to him.
review_status: accepted
```

The passage makes the procedure for the ensuing discourse an explicit topic of negotiation between speaker and audience.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0082
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 321e-322a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt

  text_sha256: bcf819da3d1a32a32ddaef5044ee45fb13e65dc2ca3cece36853da7f8efded01
greek_terms: ["θείας μετέσχε μοίρας", "διὰ τὴν τοῦ θεοῦ συγγένειαν"]
english_gloss: "shared in a divine portion ... through kinship with the god"
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_2286
feature_label: divine_moira_as_transition
observation: |
  At 321e-322a, the narrative marks the transition from human helplessness to human advancement with the phrase "the human being shared in a divine portion" (theias metesche moiras). This divine share is said to result directly in religious practice: humans alone among animals believed in gods and attempted to establish altars and images of gods. The text ties this impulse to "kinship with the god" (dia ten tou theou sungeneian).
textual_basis: |
  The passage explicitly states that because the human being shared a divine portion (theias metesche moiras), and through kinship with the god (dia ten tou theou sungeneian), humans alone among living things believed in gods and set up altars and images. The divine portion forms a hinge between the material arts stolen by Prometheus and the subsequent human cultural achievements.
limits: |
  This observation does not claim that the divine portion is identical to the stolen arts; the text distinguishes the divine share from the crafts of Hephaestus and Athena given by Prometheus. It does not address whether the divine portion is a separate gift or an effect of the stolen crafts.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0084

  - δίκην

feature_id: feature_candidate_2367
feature_label: mythic_origin_of_political_virtue
observation: "In Protagoras's Great Speech, the political art is introduced within a mythic narrative at 322b-322c as the missing condition for human city-founding: humans possess the crafts for sustenance but lack the political art, causing mutual injustice and dispersal. The speech explicitly marks this as a mythic etiology—Zeus, fearing the destruction of the human race, sends Hermes to bring aidos and dike to humans so that cities may have order and bonds of friendship."
textual_basis: "At 322b, the narrative states that humans lacked the political art, which is why they wronged one another and scattered. At 322c, Zeus intervenes mythically by sending Hermes with aidos and dike as ordering principles and bonds of friendship for cities. The entire passage is cast as a mythic narrative: Prometheus's earlier gifts (fire, crafts) are insufficient, and only a divine gift of political qualities enables civic life."
limits: "This observation identifies the mythic framing of the origin of political virtue. It does not evaluate whether Protagoras intends the myth as literal history, allegory, or rhetorical device, nor does it address how the myth relates to the subsequent logical argument about the teachability of virtue."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  Protagoras restates the central question he will address: whether political
  virtue is teachable, and he describes the popular view (that everyone partakes
  of it and that all are accepted as advisers about it) before pivoting to his
  own thesis that it is not by nature but learned.
textual_basis: |
  At 323c Protagoras signals the argument structure: he has just argued that
  everyone is reasonably accepted as an adviser about political virtue because
  they think all partake of it; he now announces he will attempt to prove that
  it is not by nature or chance but teachable and comes through care. The
  segment sets up the inquiry program for the speech.
limits: |
  This observation only notes the thesis restatement and procedural pivot. It
  does not assess the argument's validity or scope beyond the announced claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: argument_from_punishment

feature_label: forward_looking_punishment
observation: "Protagoras argues at 324a-b that rational punishment is forward-looking, aiming at prospective deterrence and education, rather than backward-looking retribution for past acts. He claims punishment is undertaken for the sake of the future, not because of the past wrongdoing."
textual_basis: "At 324a-b, Protagoras states that no one punishes a wrongdoer simply because he did wrong (unless acting like a beast without reason). The one who punishes with reason does not punish for the sake of past wrongdoing, since what is done cannot be made undone, but for the sake of the future, to prevent recurrence by both the wrongdoer and witnesses. He concludes this shows people hold virtue to be teachable, since punishment serves deterrence."
limits: "This is Protagoras's argument within his long speech, not Socratic elenchus. The observation records a rhetorical claim, not an established philosophical position. The argument's validity is not assessed."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0094
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 323a-323b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt

greek_terms:
  - τεκμήριον
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_2402
feature_label: political_virtue_unlike_demiourgic_techne
observation: "Protagoras contrasts the social response to false expertise claims in technical crafts versus political virtue. In crafts such as flute-playing, someone who falsely claims expertise is ridiculed, treated with hostility, and admonished as mad by their own household. In political virtue (justice), by contrast, someone known to be unjust who tells the truth about their own injustice before many people is considered mad, and everyone must claim to be just regardless of whether they are."
textual_basis: "At 323a-323b, Protagoras introduces a proof (tekmerion) contrasting two domains. In 'other virtues,' specifically technical crafts like flute-playing, false claims provoke ridicule, hostility, and family admonishment as mad. In justice and political virtue, the norm is reversed: truthful self-disclosure of injustice is called madness, and everyone must profess justice regardless of the truth. Protagoras presents this asymmetry as evidence that political virtue is shared by all, unlike technical crafts."
limits: "This observation records the structural contrast Protagoras draws between craft domains and political virtue. It does not evaluate whether Protagoras's argument is valid or whether the contrast holds across all of Plato's dialogues."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: "No accepted replacement is added because the durable content is already recorded by adjacent accepted observations."
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation: |
  Protagoras describes paternal moral instruction operating through oppositional pairs: just/unjust, fine/shameful, pious/impious, with an imperative to do some things and not others.
textual_basis: |
  At 325d, fathers teach and indicate at every deed and word that one thing is just, another unjust, one thing fine, another shameful, one thing pious, another impious, and instruct the child to do some things and not others. Six oppositional terms are named as pairs.
limits: |
  This is reported by Protagoras as part of his argument about the teachability of virtue; it is not Socrates' own characterization of moral education. The observation records the use of oppositional pairs as a definitional teaching method, not an endorsement of any particular moral content.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: |
  Protagoras compares the correction of a disobedient child to straightening a bent and warped piece of wood, using threats and blows.
textual_basis: |
  At 325d, if the child does not willingly obey, fathers straighten the child with threats and blows just as one straightens a bent and warped piece of wood. The analogy maps a woodworking craft process (straightening) onto the moral correction of a child.
limits: |
  This is a craft analogy within Protagoras's speech, used to illustrate the universality of moral training. It does not directly claim that education is a craft, only that correction is analogous to a craft process. The analogy is reported speech, not Socrates' own framing.

```

```yaml
observation: |
  Protagoras reports that when fathers send children to teachers, they command the teachers to care much more for the good order of the children than for letters and lyre-playing.
textual_basis: |
  At 325d-325e, after the paternal correction phase, fathers send children to teachers and command them to care far more for the orderly conduct of the children than for letters and lyre-playing. This establishes a ranked criterion in which ethical formation takes priority over technical skills.
limits: |
  This is reported within Protagoras's larger argument that virtue is taught universally. The ranking is presented as a social practice, not as a philosophical position on the value of different subjects. The observation records the priority claim, not an endorsement of it.

```

```yaml
observation: "Protagoras argues that Athenian parents treat virtue as teachable in the same way they treat other teachable subjects such as expertise (technemata): they start teaching it from earliest childhood and continue throughout life, employing multiple instructors (nurse, mother, paidagogos, father)."
textual_basis: "At 325b-c Protagoras asks Socrates to observe that, since the Athenians' punitive practices (death, exile, confiscation) presuppose that virtue is teachable, it follows that they do in fact teach it. He then lists the agents who begin instruction as soon as the child understands speech: nurse, mother, paidagogos, and the father himself."
limits: "This observation records the analogy Protagoras draws between teaching virtue and teaching other skills; it does not assess whether the analogy holds or whether the teaching practices described are effective."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0111

greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_145
feature_label: analogy_proposed_as_counterfactual
observation: "Protagoras constructs a counterfactual analogy between universal virtue-teaching and a hypothetical scenario of universal flute-playing (aulos) instruction to argue that universal eagerness to teach a subject does not guarantee that the sons of good practitioners will become good practitioners themselves."
textual_basis: "At 327b, Protagoras states that if they had the same eagerness and lack of jealousy in teaching each other flute-playing as they do for justice and virtue, the outcomes would still depend on natural aptitude rather than parentage. The analogy is explicitly marked as counterfactual through the conditional construction and is used to explain why virtue does not reliably pass from virtuous fathers to sons despite universal teaching."
limits: "This observation notes the structure of the analogy as counterfactual; it does not evaluate whether the analogy is sound or whether the aulos-to-virtue mapping is valid."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Protagoras delimits the scope of universal teaching by introducing natural aptitude as the decisive factor that determines whether a learner becomes distinguished or obscure, even under conditions of universal eager instruction."
textual_basis: "At 327b-c, Protagoras states that even if everyone taught flute-playing eagerly, the son who is most naturally gifted for flute-playing would become distinguished, while the ungifted would be obscure. He further notes that good flute-players often produce bad sons and vice versa, but all would be competent relative to complete laymen. These qualifications delimit what universal teaching can achieve."
limits: "This observation identifies the scope-limitation move within Protagoras's argument; it does not assess whether the distinction between natural aptitude and teaching is philosophically sound or whether it applies equally to virtue and to crafts."
```

```yaml

feature_label: craft_learning_produces_practitioner
observation: >-
  Protagoras argues that the sons of craftsmen learn their father's craft
  naturally and that finding a teacher for anyone inexperienced in that craft
  is difficult, using this as an analogy for how virtue is transmitted:
  everyone already teaches virtue, so a specialist teacher is not required in
  the same way.
textual_basis: >-
  In 328a Protagoras says even if one looked for someone to teach the sons of
  manual craftsmen the very craft they learned from their father, it would be
  hard to find a teacher for them, but easy for the inexperienced; he then
  applies this pattern to virtue, ending with the claim that if someone can
  advance us a little toward virtue, that is welcome.
limits: >-
  The observation notes the structural analogy between craft-learning and
  virtue-acquisition but does not evaluate whether Protagoras' claim about
  universal virtue-teaching is consistent with his earlier argument that
  virtue can be taught.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0114

  - κατὰ παράδειγμα

feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_2660
feature_label: writing_instruction_analogy_for_law_obedience
observation: "In 326c-d Protagoras maps the city's enforcement of law-learning onto the practice of writing instructors, who trace letters for students to copy. The city traces out laws, discoveries of good and ancient lawgivers, and compels citizens to rule and be ruled according to them."
textual_basis: "The passage begins with the physical education context (wealthy families sending sons early to trainers to prevent bodily cowardice in war and other actions) and then shifts at 326d to the civic-education stage: the city compels learning of the laws and living according to a pattern. The explicit simile compares this to writing instructors who trace lines for children to copy, then the city having traced out laws. This is a sustained craft analogy where the city acts like a writing teacher and the laws are the traced model."
limits: "The analogy is to writing instruction, not to all crafts. It does not claim that laws are themselves a techne, only that the city uses a pedagogical technique analogous to the writing-master's. The observation does not evaluate whether this analogy is valid."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: >
  Protagoras uses a counterfactual analogy: if a city could not exist unless
  everyone were flute-players, then everyone would teach and correct everyone
  else in flute-playing without begrudging it, just as they now do with
  justice and lawful behavior.
textual_basis: >
  At 327a, Protagoras constructs a hypothetical scenario: if a city could not
  exist unless everyone were flute-players to the best of their ability
  (326e-327a), then everyone would teach and reprove anyone playing badly,
  without begrudging it — just as now no one begrudges sharing what is just
  and lawful (327a).
limits: >
  The analogy is illustrative, not a proof; Protagoras uses it to explain an
  apparent counterexample (sons of good fathers turning out base) rather than
  to establish the teachability thesis directly.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: socratic_procedure_explained
observation: At 329b Socrates characterizes Protagoras as capable both of delivering long speeches and of answering questions briefly while waiting for and receiving the answer, adding that few have prepared themselves for this. Socrates then states he needs one small thing to have everything and requests a concise answer about whether virtue is one thing with parts or many distinct things.
textual_basis: Socrates says Protagoras (or his answer ability) is something 'few have prepared' at 329b, then frames his own request as a 'small' missing piece, implicitly constraining the exchange toward short-answer procedure.
limits: The observation does not establish whether Protagoras actually accepts or rejects the short-answer procedure; the passage prepares an explicit procedural framing.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: "Socrates proposes a joint thesis—that justice is pious and piety is just—and offers to answer for both himself and Protagoras; Protagoras objects that the matter is not so simple, and Socrates then explicitly refuses the 'if you wish' and 'if it seems to you' testing standard, insisting on a procedure that tests the actual speaker (himself and Protagoras) rather than a hypothetical concession."
textual_basis: "At 331b Socrates states he would answer on behalf of both himself and Protagoras, if permitted. Protagoras rejects the simple identification at 331b (it does not seem to him so simple as to concede that justice is pious and piety just; something in it seems to him to be different). At 331c Socrates rejects the 'if you wish' and 'if it seems to you' test and insists on testing 'me and you,' framing this as the best way to conduct the argument."
limits: "This observation identifies a procedural negotiation about who and what is being tested; it does not assess whether Socrates is being ironic or whether the thesis is true. The passage continues beyond 331c, but the procedural shift is complete within the segment."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 331b Socrates says he would affirm that justice is pious and piety just; that justice (dikaiotes) is either the same as piety (hosiotes) or most similar, and that of all things justice is most like piety and piety most like justice. This is a positive definitional proposal—either identity or extreme similarity—not merely an example."
limits: "Socrates presents this as what he would answer, not necessarily as his settled view. The observation notes the proposal of a definitional relationship between two abstract terms; it does not track whether the proposal is later revised or refuted."
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 331c Socrates says he has no need for the 'if you wish' and 'if it seems to you' to be tested, but rather 'me and you'; and he says he means this thinking it is the best way to test the argument. limits: "The observation identifies a named dialectical procedure; it does not assess whether Socrates is being ironic in rejecting the 'if you wish' standard or whether this is a veiled criticism of Protagoras's earlier concessions. The ellipsis at the end of the segment continues beyond 331c."
```

```yaml
feature_label: piety_constraint_on_inquiry
observation: When the hypothetical interrogator asks whether piety itself is pious or impious, Socrates declares he would be indignant and responds with a religious caution, refusing to entertain the possibility that piety could be impious on the grounds that nothing else would be pious if piety itself were not.
textual_basis: At 330d Socrates says he would be indignant at the question and would answer "Speak auspiciously, man; hardly would anything else be pious if piety itself were not." The response treats the question as religiously improper and constrains the scope of admissible inquiry.
limits: This observation records the piety-based constraint on the question; it does not claim that Socrates is making a substantive argument about the nature of piety, only that he marks the question as religiously unacceptable.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "After recounting Protagoras's Great Speech at length, Socrates pivots at 328e from convinced to announcing one small impasse. The deferral is explicit: Protagoras will easily supply the additional teaching, because he already expounded the many large points (328e). This marks a transition from reported speech back to live dialectical questioning."
limits: "The observation does not assess whether the difficulty is genuinely small or is Socratic irony. It also does not claim this is the first appearance of aporia in the dialogue; earlier moments such as the Hippocrates scene or the Simonides excursus are outside this span."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: turn_geometry

feature_label: respondent_compared_to_scripted_object
observation: "Socrates compares public orators to written books and to struck bronze vessels (329a): they cannot answer back or ask questions on their own, they only ring on when prompted about what has already been said, and they stop only when someone grabs hold."
textual_basis: "At 329a Socrates draws a double simile: the orators are like books that have no power to answer or to question, and like bronze vessels that, once struck, ring long and stretch out unless someone lays hold of them. Both similes contrast scripted or mechanical repetition with live dialectical exchange — exactly the turn-taking contest between extended display speech and short-answer questioning that structures the dialogue."
limits: "The observation is limited to Socrates's characterization of orators at 329a. It does not claim that Protagoras himself is being directly compared to a book or a bronze vessel here, though the juxtaposition is pointed because this comes immediately after Protagoras's long speech. Whether Socrates's remark functions as a procedural rebuke or an invitation to short-answer exchange belongs to a larger pattern visible across the dialogue and is not adjudicated here."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: elenchus

feature_label: principle_stated_then_applied_to_refute
observation: >
  Socrates secures Protagoras's assent to the general principle that each thing
  has exactly one opposite (one to one, not many), then immediately recapitulates
  the agreed premises in order to apply the principle to the earlier concessions
  about temperance and folly.
textual_basis: >
  At 332c Socrates leads Protagoras through a series of examples (beautiful/ugly,
  good/bad, high-pitched/low-pitched) and then secures the general principle
  "each of the opposites has one opposite only and not many." At 332d Socrates
  says "let us reckon up what has been agreed" and restates the earlier premises
  that foolish action is opposite to temperate action, preparing to derive a
  contradiction.
limits: >
  This record identifies the structure of the elenchus (principle then application)
  but does not evaluate whether the principle is sound or whether Protagoras
  could have resisted it. It does not claim that the refutation succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: agreement_recapitulation
observation: >
  At 332d Socrates explicitly calls for a recapitulation of the agreed-upon
  premises ("let us reckon up what has been agreed by us") before applying
  them to the case at hand (temperance and folly).
textual_basis: >
  At 332d Socrates says: "Come then, let us reckon up what has been agreed by
  us." He then restates three premises in sequence: (1) one thing has one
  opposite only, not many; (2) what is done oppositely is done by opposites;
  (3) what is done foolishly is done oppositely to what is done temperately.
limits: >
  This identifies the structural move of recapitulation within the elenchus.
  It does not assess whether the recapitulation is fair or whether the premises
  were genuinely agreed, nor does it claim that recapitulation is unique to
  this passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: >
  Before stating the one-opposite principle, Socrates secures Protagoras's
  assent through three parallel question-pairs (beautiful/ugly, good/bad,
  high-pitched/low-pitched), each asserting that there is one and only one
  opposite in each case. The accumulation of analogous pairs prepares the
  move from particular examples to the general principle.
textual_basis: >
  At 332c Socrates asks in sequence: "Is there something beautiful?" (yes),
  "Is there any opposite to this except the ugly?" (no); then the same for
  good/bad; then for sharp/flat in voice. Only after these three pairs does
  he ask: "So each single one of the opposites has one opposite only, and
  not many?" and Protagoras agrees.
limits: >
  This identifies the pattern of using multiple analogous pairs to build
  toward a general principle. It does not assess whether the pairs are
  genuinely analogous or whether Protagoras could have challenged the
  induction. It also does not claim this pattern occurs only here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_418
feature_label: recapitulation_of_objections
observation: "Socrates recapitulates the prior agreements that generated the contradiction: (1) foolishness is opposite to wisdom (from earlier); (2) foolishness is opposite to sound-mindedness (just deduced); (3) one thing has only one opposite. Since wisdom and sound-mindedness (sophrosyne) would both be opposite to foolishness, the three premises cannot all stand together."
textual_basis: "At 332e Socrates explicitly recalls the earlier agreement that aphrosyne is opposite to sophia (me mnesai... aphrosyne sophiai enantion einai) then adds the established premise that one thing has only one opposite (hen de heni monon enantion einai, 333a). He states the two logoi are discordant (ou synaidousin oude synarmottousin allelois)."
limits: "This observation records the recapitulation of an objection, not a resolution. The passage does not decide whether the 'one opposite' principle or the earlier Protagorean claim about the parts of virtue is to be abandoned."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 333d Protagoras initially resists answering (he blamed the argument for being difficult), then concedes to answer. Socrates proceeds through a chain: do some who act unjustly seem to be temperate? (granted); by 'temperate' do you mean 'think well'? (yes); by 'think well' do you mean 'deliberate well' because they act unjustly? (granted); do they do well or badly? (do well); so you say there are some good things? (yes); are those things good which are beneficial to human beings? Protagoras agrees but adds a qualification at 333e."
limits: "The observation records the assent-chain cadence and Protagoras's shift from resistance to assent; it does not evaluate the logical validity of the chain or claim that the chain refutes a specific doctrine."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0152

  - ἀγαθόν
  - ποικίλον
english_gloss: "good, variable/many-colored"
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1877
feature_label: definition_by_relational_opposition
observation: "Protagoras elaborates on the good by arguing that the same thing can be good for one recipient and bad for another, or good for one part of an organism and harmful for another, introducing a domain-relative and part-relative account of goodness."
textual_basis: "At 334a, Protagoras responds to Socrates with a catalogue of examples: foods, drinks, and drugs are beneficial to some creatures but not to others; manure is good for plant roots but destroys shoots and young branches; olive oil is bad for all plants and for the hair of every animal except humans, for whom it is beneficial to hair and the rest of the body. He concludes at 334b that the good is variable and manifold (polychrome)."
limits: "The passage develops a relational, not a substantive, definition of the good. It does not specify whether the good is unitary or whether such variability precludes a single definition. Protagoras does not here apply the relational schema to justice or piety."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Protagoras delivers a sustained set-speech in response to a question about the good, deploying a dense catalogue of examples across recipient domains rather than offering a concise definition or engaging in short-answer dialectic."
textual_basis: "From 334a to 334b, Protagoras speaks without interruption, producing an extended reply that surveys foods, drinks, drugs, manure, plant parts, olive oil, animal hair, and human bodily care, concluding with a generalizing maxim about the variability of the good. The passage exhibits no question-answer alternation and is delivered as a continuous rhetorical exposition."
limits: "The observation notes the turn form (extended response) but does not evaluate whether this is evasion, rhetorical display, or pedagogical choice. It does not claim that Protagoras is avoiding the inquiry."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: truth_priority_procedure
observation: >
  Socrates declares a procedural principle at 333c: what matters most is
  examining the logos (argument), not the personal beliefs of the respondent.
  He adds that, incidentally, both the questioner and the answerer are also
  tested through the examination.
textual_basis: >
  At 333c Socrates says he examines the logos most of all, and that it
  perhaps turns out that both the questioner and the answerer are examined.
  This is a meta-procedural statement about the priority of argument over
  personal commitment.
limits: >
  Does not claim that Socrates always follows this principle; records only this
  explicit statement at 333c. The hedging ("perhaps") with respect to the
  examination of persons is noted but not analyzed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms: ["βραχέα", "βραχυλογίᾳ", "βραχυτέροις", "μακρὰ λέγειν"]
english_gloss: "short; brevity; in shorter; speak at length"
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_1128
feature_label: respondent_answer_constraint
observation: >
  Socrates requests that Protagoras adopt brevity as the exclusive mode for their
  discussion, framing the choice as between two available styles: Protagoras's
  capacity for extended speech versus his capacity for extreme concision.
textual_basis: >
  After Protagoras asks whether Socrates wants answers of the length Protagoras
  thinks necessary or of the length Socrates thinks necessary, Socrates replies by
  acknowledging Protagoras's ability both to speak at length without ever running
  out of logos and to speak so briefly that no one could speak in fewer words
  (334e-335a). He then demands that Protagoras use the latter mode, brevity
  (brachulogia), for their exchange.
limits: >
  This record notes the procedural demand for a specific respondent style;
  it does not assess whether Protagoras actually complies or whether Socrates
  later relaxes the constraint.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  Protagoras ties his established reputation among the Greeks to his refusal

  that had he always yielded to the opponent's preferred mode, he would have
  surpassed no one and his name would not have become known among the Greeks.
textual_basis: >
  At the end of 334e-335a, Protagoras responds to Socrates's demand for brevity
  by stating that he has entered many contests of speeches and that if he had
  always conversed as the opponent demanded, he would have appeared better than
  no one and the name of Protagoras would not have arisen among the Greeks.
limits: >
  This record notes the self-advertisement linking reputation to procedural

  this claim is challenged later in the dialogue.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0161
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 335b-335c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt
  stephanus_span: 335b-335c
  start_marker: 335b
  end_marker: 335c
  start_char: 53090
  end_char: 54010
  text_sha256: "4a7c9c2660859bf939fb9ce30332ae1f5d28c99cc47fe941e90a71b1364b05bb"
greek_terms:
  - σοφὸς γὰρ εἶ
english_gloss: "for you are wise"
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_260
feature_label: mock_deference
observation: >
  Socrates addresses Protagoras with a parenthetical "for you are wise" (335c) and credits him with mastery of both long and short speeches, while claiming his own inability with long speeches. This praise is deployed in the context of Socrates announcing his departure from the gathering because Protagoras refuses to adopt a conversational format Socrates can follow.
textual_basis: >
  At 335b-c Socrates says that Protagoras, as is said of him and as he himself claims, is capable of conducting conversations in both long speech (makrologia) and short speech (brachylogia). He inserts the parenthetical "for you are wise" (sophos gar ei) at 335c, then contrasts this with his own inability (adynatos) with long speeches. The praise immediately precedes Socrates' announcement that he will leave, making the deference function as a polite cover for his procedural ultimatum.
limits: >
  Does not establish that Socrates' praise is insincere; only identifies the structural pattern in which praise of an interlocutor's versatility is paired with self-disavowal in a departure threat. Does not claim Socrates secretly intends to mock Protagoras.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἀνιστάμην
  - ἐπιλαμβάνεται
english_gloss: "I was rising ... he seizes"
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_1119
feature_label: dramatic_disruption_or_pause
observation: >
  Socrates physically rises to leave the gathering (335c), and as he is rising Callias seizes his right hand to detain him. The procedural impasse over conversational format escalates into embodied action that physically disrupts the discussion and requires physical intervention from a third party.
textual_basis: >
  At 335c Socrates narrates "and at the same time having said these things I was rising (anistamen) as if departing." Immediately, "as I was rising, Callias seizes (epilambanetai) my hand with his right hand." The departure threat is not merely verbal but enacted through physical movement, and the response is likewise a physical restraint.
limits: >
  Does not establish Socrates' sincerity in intending to leave. Records only the dramatic structure: verbal procedural impasse followed by physical enactment of departure, met by physical restraint from a third party. Does not claim this is a planned rhetorical tactic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0163
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 335b-335c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt
  stephanus_span: 335b-335c
  start_marker: 335b
  end_marker: 335c
  start_char: 53090
  end_char: 54010
  text_sha256: "4a7c9c2660859bf939fb9ce30332ae1f5d28c99cc47fe941e90a71b1364b05bb"
greek_terms:
  - διαλέγεσθαι
  - ἕπεσθαι
english_gloss: "to converse ... to follow"
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: >
  Socrates states a procedural condition for the dialogue to continue: Protagoras must be willing to converse in a manner that Socrates can follow. Socrates frames his inability with long speeches not as a refusal but as a capacity constraint, and makes the continuation of the gathering conditional on Protagoras accommodating this constraint since Protagoras has both capacities.
textual_basis: >
  At 335b Socrates says: "whenever you wish to converse (dialegesthai) as I am able to follow (hespesthai), then I will converse with you." He then states at 335c that Protagoras ought to accommodate (sygchorein) since he is capable of both long and short speech, while Socrates cannot handle long speeches. Socrates then announces his departure, making the procedural condition an ultimatum.
limits: >
  Does not evaluate whether Socrates' claimed inability with long speeches is genuine or tactical. Records only the explicit procedural condition stated by the speaker and its function in shaping the continuation or termination of the discussion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: "forgetful, I forget, cut short, shorter"
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: >
  Socrates, responding to Hippocrates' silence after Protagoras' long speech,
  requests that Protagoras shorten his answers because Socrates claims to be
  forgetful; he analogizes his forgetfulness to deafness, saying that just as a
  deaf person would need louder speech, he needs briefer answers.
textual_basis: >
  At 334c-d Socrates says 'I happen to be a forgetful person, and if someone
  speaks at length to me, I forget what the speech is about' and explicitly
  requests 'cut short your answers and make them shorter, if I am to follow
  you.' This is a procedural intervention to control turn length, anchored in
  an existing feature label for macrology restraint.
limits: >
  The observation records Socrates' procedural move against long speeches; it
  does not assess whether the forgetfulness claim is sincere or ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - φαύλῳ
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker

feature_label: mock_deference
observation: "Socrates tells the company that in truth it is impossible for them to choose anyone wiser than Protagoras, then immediately describes the alternative — pretending to select someone who is no better — as shameful, as if choosing an overseer for a worthless man. The argument both elevates Protagoras hyperbolically and recasts the whole selection procedure as ridiculous."
textual_basis: "At 338c Socrates says in truth it is impossible for you to choose anyone wiser than Protagoras here, then treats any merely nominal selection as shameful, as if appointing an overseer for a worthless man. The comparison of a non-superior choice to appointing an overseer for a worthless man doubles as mock-deference to Protagoras while undermining the whole procedure."
limits: "This observation registers the ironic praise of Protagoras and the dismissive framing of the selection procedure, but does not claim that Socrates secretly believes Protagoras is not wise or that the irony is directed at a hidden target."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_260
feature_label: mock_deference
observation: Socrates characterizes Prodicus's wisdom as "divine" and traces its origin to Simonides "or even earlier," attributing to a living sophist a quasi-mythic pedigree in the context of a dispute over interpreting a Simonides poem.
textual_basis: Socrates opens 341a with the claim that Prodicus's wisdom is a certain divine thing (theia tis), then traces it to Simonides or still more ancient sources. The hyperbolic attribution of divinity and archaic lineage to a contemporary sophist's word-distinction practice is a textbook instance of mock-deference.
limits: This observation notes the rhetorical posture of the passage, not whether Socrates genuinely reveres Prodicus. It does not assess the substance of Prodicus's word-distinctions.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 341c Socrates asks Prodicus what Simonides means by the term under dispute and Prodicus answers with the term meaning 'bad'. Socrates immediately applies this equivalence to interpret Simonides' critique of Pittacus. Earlier at 341b the argument is prepared by establishing that the term meaning 'terrible' means 'bad' through a test of which nouns it combines with (sickness, war, poverty but not wealth, peace, health)."
limits: "The observation does not claim that this semantic equivalence is correct or that Plato endorses Prodicus' view; it records the textual move of treating one term as a synonym for another to resolve a poetic puzzle."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
stephanus_span: 340d-340e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt
  stephanus_span: 340d-340e
  start_marker: 340d
  end_marker: 340e
  start_char: 63888
  end_char: 64761
  text_sha256: beb037efcc89821d9c4a60bc200ecd997bffab5d234d58e2ac8a60a79f28d144
greek_terms:
  - ἰατρός
  - ἰώμενος
  - νόσημα
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_043
feature_label: expert_craft_analogy
observation: Socrates deploys a medical craft analogy to characterize his argumentative failure, casting himself as a doctor whose attempted remedy worsens the disease.
textual_basis: At 340d-e, Socrates responds to Protagoras's critique by saying he is a ridiculous doctor whose healing makes the disease greater, mapping the craft of medicine onto dialectical correction.
limits: The analogy is used self-deprecatingly and does not develop a sustained craft-domain mapping; it functions as a rhetorical concession rather than an argumentative analogy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - παίζειν
  - ἀποπειρᾶσθαι
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_3076
feature_label: socratic_attribution_of_play
observation: "Socrates attributes play to Prodicus, claiming Prodicus is joking and pretending to test whether Protagoras can defend his own argument, rather than genuinely holding the interpretive position Prodicus has just advanced."
textual_basis: "At 341d, Socrates says Prodicus is playing and seeming to test whether Protagoras will be able to defend his own argument (paizein kai sou dokein apopeirasthai ei hoios t' esē tōi sautou logōi boēthein). This explicitly reframes a serious-seeming interpretive intervention as playful testing."
limits: "Does not establish whether Prodicus is actually playing or Socrates is mischaracterizing him; records only the in-text attribution of play."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - τεκμήριον
  - τὸ μετὰ τοῦτο ῥῆμα
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: hermeneutic_method
feature_id: feature_candidate_3077
feature_label: immediate_context_as_proof
observation: "Socrates deploys a specific method of poetic interpretation, using the immediately subsequent phrase as a tekmērion (proof) that Simonides could not have intended a particular meaning for chalepon."
textual_basis: "At 341d-341e, Socrates announces a mega tekmērion (great proof) drawn from to meta touto rhēma (the very next phrase): Simonides writes that a god alone would have this honor. Socrates argues Simonides would not call it bad to become good and then assign this honor exclusively to a god, so chalepon cannot mean kakon here."
limits: "Records only the hermeneutic method Socrates claims to deploy; does not assess the validity of the interpretation or whether Socrates endorses this method in general."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_427
feature_label: poetic_citation_as_argument
observation: "Socrates quotes Homer's Iliad — Scamander calling on Simoeis for help against Achilles — and explicitly applies it as a parallel for his own action of calling on Prodicus to help defend Simonides' poem against Protagoras's interpretation."
textual_basis: "At 339e-340a, Socrates introduces the Homeric quotation with the phrase marking it as Homer's words and then marks the application with 'and I too call upon you,' making the Homeric scene an analogue for his procedural move of enlisting Prodicus."
limits: "This observation identifies the quotation and its framing function. It does not assess the validity of the analogy or the accuracy of Socrates' use of Homer."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates describes his own reaction to Protagoras's speech in vivid physical terms: he was 'darkened and dizzy' as if struck by a good boxer, with the others adding their clamorous approval."
textual_basis: "At 339e, Socrates says he was struck as if by a good boxer and that he experienced darkness and dizziness, while the audience added their uproar. The boxer metaphor is self-deprecating."
limits: "This observation identifies the self-deprecating physical metaphor. It does not claim Socrates was literally incapacitated or assess the sincerity of the description."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - σὸς μέντοι Σιμωνίδης πολίτης
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: "Socrates, feeling overwhelmed by Protagoras's critique of his Simonides interpretation, turns away from the direct exchange and calls on Prodicus to take up the defense, justifying the transfer by noting Prodicus shares citizenship with Simonides and is therefore obliged to help."
textual_basis: "At 339e-340a, Socrates says he turns toward Prodicus and addresses him with the claim that Simonides is his fellow citizen and that he is justly obliged to help the man. The appeal to shared citizenship serves as the authority hedge for the procedural transfer."
limits: "This observation identifies the procedural move and its justification. It does not assess whether Prodicus accepts the role or whether the transfer succeeds."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: respondent_answer_constraint
observation: "Alcibiades constrains Protagoras' respondent role: if Protagoras claims equality in dialogue, he must converse by asking and answering rather than stretching each answer into a long speech."
textual_basis: "At 336c-336d Alcibiades says Protagoras should engage by asking and answering, not lengthening each answer into a long speech that knocks aside the arguments and makes many listeners forget the question."
limits: "This records Alcibiades' procedural constraint on answer form. It does not include Critias' later intervention."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0201
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 336d-336e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt
  stephanus_span: 336d-336e
  start_marker: 336d
  end_marker: 336e
  start_char: 55829
  end_char: 56432
  text_sha256: f231ece0fa96626b171d0cdd7e22fbca16e95fc779d5d79d5a0e9f621c73b9d1
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: prosopography
feature_id: feature_candidate_349
feature_label: character_description_by_speaker
observation: |
  Critias, speaking after Alcibiades, diagnoses the partisan alignment of the other named participants: Callias is strongly on Protagoras's side, while Alcibiades is always contentious (philonikos) toward whatever he pursues. He then proposes that he, Prodicus, and Hippias should jointly request both Socrates and Protagoras not to dissolve the meeting midway (336d-e).
textual_basis: |
  Critias explicitly names and characterizes Callias (mala pros Protagorou einai, strongly pro-Protagoras) and Alcibiades (aei philonikos, always fond of contention). He then distances himself and the other sophists from the partisanship (hemas de ouden dei sumphilonikein) and proposes a joint procedural request.
limits: |
  Records Critias's diagnosis of factional alignment and his procedural response. Does not evaluate the accuracy of his characterizations or whether his proposal is followed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0202
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 336d-336e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt
  stephanus_span: 336d-336e
  start_marker: 336d
  end_marker: 336e

  end_char: 56432
  text_sha256: f231ece0fa96626b171d0cdd7e22fbca16e95fc779d5d79d5a0e9f621c73b9d1
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: |
  Critias proposes that the neutral parties (himself, Prodicus, and Hippias) jointly ask both Socrates and Protagoras not to dissolve the meeting midway (336e). This is a procedural intervention aimed at preventing the discussion from breaking down.
textual_basis: |
  Critias explicitly states that they should jointly (koine) request (deisthai) both parties not to dissolve the gathering midway (me metaxu dialusai ten sunousian). This frames the procedural risk as a premature dissolution and recruits neutral parties to prevent it.
limits: |
  Records a procedural plea to continue, not a substantive argument. Does not assess whether the meeting in fact continues or whether the plea is effective.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: prosopography
feature_id: feature_candidate_3092
feature_label: prodicus_semantic_authority
observation: Socrates appeals to Prodicus to confirm that "to become" and "to be" are distinct.
textual_basis: At 340b, Socrates addresses Prodicus directly, asking whether "to become" and "to be" are the same or different, and Prodicus answers that they are different.
limits: This observation records only that Socrates invokes Prodicus as an authority on semantic distinctions in this passage; it does not claim this is the only function Prodicus serves in the dialogue or in Plato generally.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0204

  text_sha256: aabde6d4db5c72a83464218a4b470cdb894081a46394f115794a34d107995cb5
greek_terms: ["γενέσθαι", "εἶναι", "ἔμμεναι"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_3093
feature_label: semantic_distinction_resolves_contradiction
observation: Socrates uses the distinction between "to become" and "to be" to argue that Simonides does not contradict himself when he claims it is hard to become good while criticizing Pittacus for claiming it is hard to be good.
textual_basis: At 340c, Socrates argues that since being and becoming are not the same, Simonides, who spoke of becoming good, does not contradict himself when criticizing Pittacus, who spoke of being good.
limits: This observation records the elenctic move of using semantic distinction to dissolve an apparent contradiction; it does not evaluate whether the distinction genuinely resolves the contradiction or whether Socrates' interpretation of Simonides is correct.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0206
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 344c-344d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt

  end_marker: 344d

  end_char: 73078
  text_sha256: 1cdff2cc9eec8b7698dd2da82885b87ff835756b5f8fdf8771f34b48cc6b9093
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_1607
feature_label: humble_craft_as_methodological_paradigm
observation: "Socrates argues through parallel craft examples at 344c-344d: the ship-pilot struck by a great storm, the farmer by a harsh season, and the doctor by the same. Each craft exemplifies how a capable person can be rendered helpless by external misfortune."
textual_basis: "At 344c Socrates explains that an 'impossible misfortune' overthrows not the layman but the capable person, just as one cannot knock down someone already lying down. He then applies this to three crafts: the helmsman (344c-344d), farmer, and doctor (344d)."
limits: "This records the use of multiple crafts as parallel illustrations of a single argumentative point. It does not claim that these are the only crafts cited or that the argument is sound."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0207
```
