```yaml
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_3098
feature_label: force_of_analogy_chain
observation: "Socrates builds an analogical chain at 344c-344d: the image of knocking down the standing man (not the already fallen) is applied to show that misfortune overthrows only the capable, then extended to the helmsman, farmer, and doctor, concluding that 'it is possible for the good man to become bad,' supported by a second poetic testimony."
textual_basis: "At 344c the knockdown analogy introduces the principle. At 344c-344d it is extended serially to the helmsman, farmer, and doctor. At 344d the conclusion 'for the good man it is possible to become bad' is drawn and reinforced by testimony from another poet."
limits: "This records the analogical chain structure; it does not evaluate whether the chain successfully refutes Pittacus' maxim or whether the conclusion follows."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_1423
feature_label: socratic_mock_praise
observation: Socrates claims the Lacedaemonians are best educated in philosophy and speeches, a praise that inverts the common Greek perception of Spartans as laconic and anti-intellectual.
textual_basis: At 342d Socrates explicitly states that the Lacedaemonians are "best educated in philosophy and speeches," framing this as a truth claim. The surrounding passage at 342c describes Spartans using foreigner expulsions to secretly meet with sophists, presenting their anti-intellectual public image as a calculated deception.
limits: This observation notes the ironic structure of the praise claim; it does not assess whether the historical Spartans actually cultivated philosophy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: ethnographic_custom_cited_in_argument
observation: Spartan practices of foreigner expulsion and a prohibition on youth traveling to other cities are described as mechanisms for controlling access to sophists and preserving education, with a Cretan parallel noted for the youth travel restriction.
textual_basis: At 342c Socrates describes Spartans conducting foreigner expulsions to secretly associate with sophists while excluding foreign witnesses, and at 342c-342d he notes they prevent youth from traveling to other cities, citing the same Cretan practice, so the young do not unlearn what they are taught.
limits: This observation records the ethnographic customs as cited in the argument; it does not assess the historical accuracy of these Spartan and Cretan practices.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates pauses a close textual analysis of Simonides' poem to announce a shift from examining every detail to tracing the overall shape and intention of the work, explicitly framing this as a more efficient and relevant alternative to a line-by-line exposition."
textual_basis: "At 344a-b Socrates states that it would be too long to go through each point in the poem individually, and instead proposes to run through the overall outline and intention. limits: "This observation does not claim that Socrates is correct or that the poem's meaning is thereby determined; it only notes the explicit procedural shift."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  text_sha256: 046b21106e388f62eab41bada3a4fc4ecdbc579237d8d750584a7125fbcf6d7a
greek_terms: ["χαλεπὸν ἐσθλὸν ἔμμεναι", "γενέσθαι", "ἔμμεναι", "πράξας μὲν γὰρ εὖ πᾶς ἀνὴρ ἀγαθός, κακὸς δ' εἰ κακῶς"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: poetic_quotation_as_argument
feature_id: feature_candidate_1874
feature_label: poetic_quotation_as_authority
observation: Socrates disputes Pittacus' maxim by quoting Simonides' rebuttal of it. In the embedded poetic refutation at 344e, Simonides argues that Pittacus' claim that it is hard to be good (esthlon emmenai) is incorrect because being good is impossible, while becoming good is merely difficult. Simonides then offers a counter-principle at 344e: every man is good when he fares well (prattōn eu) and bad when he fares badly (kakōs).
textual_basis: At 344e Socrates frames the passage as Simonides' direct response to Pittacus' saying, quoting the counter-claim 'prattōn men gar eu pas anēr agathos, kakos d' ei kakōs' and stating that Simonides holds 'emmenai de adunaton' (being good is impossible) versus 'genesthai men chalepon, dunaton de' (becoming good is difficult but possible).
limits: This observation records a poetic-quotation refutation embedded within Socrates' speech; it does not claim the argument is valid or that Simonides' poem is accurately represented.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms: ["εὐπραγία", "μάθησις"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_1048
feature_label: craft_analogy
observation: At 345a Socrates tests Simonides' principle that good action makes someone good by applying it to craft domains. He asks what good action (eupragia) makes someone good at letters (grammata) and answers that it is learning (mathēsis). He extends the same test to medicine: the good action that makes a good doctor is learning the treatment of patients. The test shows that in crafts, what makes one good is learning, not mere success in action.
textual_basis: At 345a Socrates asks 'tis oun eis grammata agathē praxis estin, kai tis andra agathon poiei eis grammata? dēlon hoti hē toutōn mathēsis,' then parallels the structure for medicine: 'tis de eupragia agathon iatron poiei? dēlon hoti hē tōn kamnontōn tēs therapeias mathēsis.'
limits: This observation records a craft-domain test of a poetic maxim; it does not claim that this refutation succeeds or that Simonides is defeated.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates begins his interpretation by asserting that the oldest and most abundant philosophy among the Greeks is found in Crete and Sparta, and that the greatest sophists are there — but they conceal their wisdom under a facade of ignorance and military prowess, deceiving their imitators in other cities."
textual_basis: "At 342a-342b Socrates claims that philosophy is oldest and most practiced in Crete and Sparta, that the most sophists anywhere are there, and that they deliberately deny wisdom and present themselves as ignorant so as to appear to excel through fighting rather than recognized wisdom. He adds that those in other cities who imitate Spartan ways break their ears (a boxing reference) without understanding the concealed intellectual practice."
limits: "This is the opening frame of Socrates' speech about the Spartan-Cretan wisdom tradition. It does not extend to the specific poetic interpretation of Simonides' poem that follows, nor does it assess whether Socrates' claim about Spartan intellectualism is made in earnest or with irony."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0223

  text_sha256: 744597ed2f6367d5d10b986b89b2e98f20717dc98d8895f254230fb94d41e654
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: |
  Socrates proposes abandoning the discussion of poets and instead conducting
  direct dialectical examination between himself and Protagoras. He explicitly
  offers a choice of roles: he is ready to answer if Protagoras wishes to
  question, or Protagoras may answer while Socrates questions about the
  topic they had left unfinished.
textual_basis: |
  At 348a Socrates states that they should imitate those who converse by
  themselves, "putting the poets aside" (katathemenous tous poietas), and make
  speeches through themselves, testing the truth and each other. He then
  offers the two-way procedural option: if Protagoras wishes to question,
  Socrates is ready to answer, or Protagoras may provide answers so they can
  finish what they were discussing.
limits: |
  This is a procedural proposal, not a record of its acceptance. Protagoras
  does not respond in this segment, so the procedure Socrates offers is not
  yet enacted.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1130
feature_label: procedural_interruption
observation: |
  After Protagoras fails to clarify whether he will engage in the proposed
  dialectical exchange, Alcibiades intervenes. Addressing Callias rather
  than Protagoras directly, he demands that Protagoras either engage in
  dialogue or state that he refuses, so that Socrates may speak with someone
  else.
textual_basis: |
  At 348b the narrator reports that Protagoras gave no clear sign
  (ouden apesaphei) of what he would do. Alcibiades then looks at Callias
  and criticizes Protagoras in the third person for not clarifying whether
  he will give an account. He presents a forced alternative: either
  Protagoras must engage in dialogue or declare his refusal, so the group
  can proceed accordingly.
limits: |
  This records Alcibiades's intervention as a procedural move, not its
  outcome. The segment ends before any resolution is reported.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0225
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 348e-349a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt

  start_char: 81338
  end_char: 82117
  text_sha256: 450d695597d85bd731dbb5eb1e1504f1ab7a38bf5f7440cbbd1cad234c98c377
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_260
feature_label: mock_deference
observation: Socrates delivers elaborately layered praise to Protagoras at 348e-349a: he says Protagoras not only considers himself kalos kagathos but can make others good, openly proclaims himself a sophist to all the Greeks, and was the first to demand payment for teaching virtue. The accumulation of superlative claims and the pointed mention of self-advertisement and fee-charging signal that the praise is not straightforward endorsement but a setup for examination.
textual_basis: At 348e Socrates singles out Protagoras as uniquely worth consulting about virtue. At 349a he adds that Protagoras openly advertised himself to all Greeks as a sophist and teacher of virtue, and was the first to demand a fee for it. The sequence builds from flattery to an implicit challenge: the very qualities that make Protagoras worth consulting also make him accountable for defending his claims.
limits: This observation notes a rhetorical pattern; it does not determine Socrates' private beliefs about Protagoras or whether the praise is partly sincere. It does not claim that any specific subsequent refutation is already present in this passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: Socrates uses the term for craft at 348e-349a when referring to what others conceal and Protagoras openly markets. The term casts virtue-education as a technical skill with an identifiable practitioner as teacher, a method of transmission, and a fee structure.
limits: This observation identifies the craft-framing language. It does not assess whether Socrates endorses this framing or whether it survives cross-examination later in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "Socrates explicitly recapitulates at 349b-c: the five terms are onomata, each underlies a distinct ousia and pragma with its own dynamis, and all are parts of virtue, distinguished by the gold-part versus face-part analogy."
limits: "This is a recapitulation of Protagoras's earlier view, not a new claim by Socrates; it does not resolve whether the distinction stands under further examination."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: "The analogy is presented as a recapitulation of Protagoras's earlier view, not as Socrates's own substantive argument; it establishes the terms for the subsequent examination."
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0231

greek_terms:
  - "ἐπιστήμης στερηθῆναι"
  - "κακὴ πρᾶξις"
english_gloss: "deprivation of knowledge; bad action"
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_085
feature_label: knowledge_priority_argument
observation: "Socrates claims that the only bad action is deprivation of knowledge, identifying loss of knowledge as the sole mechanism by which a good man becomes bad — whether through time, toil, disease, or other mishap. A medical-craft analogy supports this: just as one cannot become a bad doctor without first being a doctor, so one cannot become bad without first having been good."
textual_basis: "At 345b, Socrates states that this alone is bad action — to be deprived of knowledge — and argues via the analogy that just as someone who is not a doctor cannot become a bad doctor, a bad man cannot become bad without first becoming good. Becoming bad is always a loss of a prior good condition grounded in knowledge."
limits: "The passage does not argue that knowledge is the whole of virtue; it claims only that becoming bad is caused by loss of a knowledge-based good condition."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates embeds two quotations from Simonides' poem at 345c and uses them as evidence in his ongoing interpretation of the poem as a critique of Pittacus, claiming that both the immediate passage and subsequent verses of the song are directed against him."
textual_basis: "At 345c, Socrates quotes two passages from Simonides' poem and states that all these things are said against Pittacus, and that the subsequent verses of the song make it even clearer."
limits: "This observation records the use of poetic quotation as argumentative evidence within the interpretation of Simonides, not whether Socrates' reading of the poem is correct."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_1174
feature_label: ethical_type_profile
observation: "Socrates draws a binary ethical type contrast between 'the base' and 'the good' based on how each type responds when something bad happens to their parents or fatherland. The base eagerly blame and criticize to avoid being blamed for neglect themselves, while the good conceal the fault, praise, and compel themselves to love and praise even when wronged."
textual_basis: "At 346a Socrates describes the base as eager to blame and criticize the wickedness of parents or fatherland (346a-346b). At 346b he contrasts this with the good, who conceal, praise, and compel themselves to reconcile and love even when wronged."
limits: "This is a general ethical typology deployed in the context of interpreting Simonides' poem. It does not claim these are Socrates' or Plato's own ethical categories, nor does it assert they map onto specific historical figures."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Protagoras maintains the parts-of-virtue account while isolating courage as the unlike case: many people may lack justice, holiness, moderation, and knowledge yet still be exceptionally courageous."
textual_basis: "At 349d, Protagoras says all the named qualities are parts of virtue, that four are fairly similar, but courage differs greatly from all the others; he then gives the case of people who are unjust, impious, intemperate, and ignorant while being extraordinarily courageous."
limits: "This records Protagoras' exception claim for courage within the virtue-parts discussion. It does not decide whether the exception is coherent."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: measurement_craft_corrects_appearance
observation: "Socrates introduces measurement as the craft that corrects misleading appearances of near and distant magnitudes, contrasting it with the power of appearance and saying it would save life in choices about larger and smaller things."
textual_basis: "At 356c-356e, Socrates asks whether equal sizes and sounds appear larger near and smaller far away, then asks whether life would be saved by the art of measurement rather than by appearance when choosing large and small things."
limits: "This records the measurement analogy as introduced within the target span. It does not include the continuation of the odd-even example beyond 356e."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0304

  start_char: 61297
  end_char: 62634
  text_sha256: 3e1e1dc878328c13523feaa6ed9b36da531f4c1ca8a623d2ae60de561d075f7f
greek_terms:
  - χαλεπὸν
  - ἐσθλὸν
  - ἔμμεναι
  - ὁμολογεῖσθαι
  - μέμφεται
english_gloss: Protagoras charges Simonides with self-contradiction within a single poem.
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_4709
feature_label: contradiction_in_quoted_authority
observation: Protagoras argues that Simonides contradicts himself: the poet first asserts it is difficult for a man to become good (339c), but later in the same poem criticizes Pittacus for saying the same thing, namely that it is hard to be good (339d). Protagoras concludes that Simonides must be wrong in either the earlier or the later statement.
textual_basis: At 339c Protagoras quotes the Simonidean line about Pittacus and asks whether it agrees with what was said before. At 339d Protagoras explicitly states that Simonides first posited it is difficult for a good man to become good, then forgot this and reproaches Pittacus for saying the same thing, so that Simonides reproaches himself.
limits: The observation concerns Protagoras' argument as reported by Socrates; it does not assess whether the charge of self-contradiction is philosophically sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates reports that after Protagoras' argument he was stunned and dizzy, comparing himself to a man struck by a skilled boxer, and that the audience erupted in applause and uproar (339e).
textual_basis: At 339e Socrates says he was struck as if by a good boxer, was darkened and dizzy, and that the others joined in the uproar. This is a first-person report of disorientation immediately following Protagoras' refutation.
limits: The observation records Socrates' self-reported reaction; it does not determine whether the disorientation is sincere or strategic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
---
observation_id: obs_protagoras_0307
source_work: Protagoras
stephanus_span: 345d-346a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/protagoras.txt

  start_marker: 345d
  end_marker: 346a
  start_char: 74898
  end_char: 76131
  text_sha256: 6811065f04bac1b03f482482ef146b87fc9b69148ec57170f641ec6da30d5b55
greek_terms:
  - ᾄσματος
  - ἐπεξέρχεται
  - Πιττακοῦ
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_4712
feature_label: poetic_quotation_as_polemical_unit
observation: >
  Socrates presents a line of Simonides' poem as a deliberate polemical attack
  on a saying of Pittacus, stating that Simonides "goes after" Pittacus's
  statement throughout the whole song (345d). The poetic quotation is thus
  treated as an argumentative unit directed against a specific philosophical
  thesis, not as autonomous lyric.
textual_basis: >
  At 345d Socrates says Simonides attacks the saying of Pittacus "through the
  whole song," then quotes a line and glosses it as spoken "against this very
  thing." The quoted verse is immediately subordinated to an interpretive claim
  about what Simonides intended to refute.
limits: >
  The observation covers how Socrates frames the quotation's function, not
  whether Simonides actually intended polemic against Pittacus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - σοφῶν
  - ἑκόντα
  - ἐξαμαρτάνειν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_4713
feature_label: wise_men_doctrine_attribution
observation: >
  Socrates attributes to the wise collectively the doctrine that no one errs
  willingly, stating that none of the wise men thinks any human voluntarily
  does wrong or willingly performs shameful deeds (345e). He frames this as
  something he "pretty much thinks" rather than as his own discovery, shifting
  authority to unnamed sophoi.
textual_basis: >
  At 345e Socrates says he "pretty much thinks" that no one among the wise men
  holds that anyone errs willingly, and that all who do shameful things do so
  unwillingly. The doctrine is presented as the consensus of the wise, not as
  Socrates' personal claim.
limits: >
  The observation records the attribution pattern, not whether Socrates
  genuinely endorses the doctrine or is being ironic about his own knowledge.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: hypothetical_compelled_praise_scenario
observation: >
  Socrates constructs a hypothetical scenario in which a noble and good man is
  compelled to become a friend and praiser of someone with a difficult parent
  or fatherland, contrasting this with bad men who eagerly seize on such
  defects to criticize (346a). The scenario is offered to explain Simonides'
  motivation for praising despite disagreement.
textual_basis: >
  At 345e-346a Socrates says Simonides thought a noble man often compels
  himself to be a friend and praiser, giving the example of a man encountering
  a strange parent or fatherland. He then contrasts the wicked, who gladly
  observe and denounce such defects.
limits: >
  The observation covers the hypothetical case and its contrast structure, not
  whether the scenario accurately reflects Simonides' actual circumstances.
review_status: accepted
```
